(00:11:32)

Good morning. My name is Layla Jelena. I am a proud participant of Girls Inc. at the Young Women's Leadership School of Astoria and I am here today to urge you to keep this life-changing program at our school. Growing up, I was often told I was too much. I have a big personality. I am loud. I speak my mind and I express myself fully. In many spaces that gets treated like a problem to be fixed. But when I walked into Girls Inc., they did not try to quiet me down — they completely celebrated it. They took me to my first ever Liberty game, gave me the space to host events, trusted me to lead groups and were proud of me when I was just being myself. Because of them I learned that my big personality is not something to be ashamed of — it is my greatest strength. That is why your funding belongs here, because you are not just paying for a school slot, you are funding the future leaders of this school. Without Girls Inc., students lose the one place where they can truly be themselves. Thank you.

(00:12:35)

My name is Zina Abbott and I am the Program Director for Girls Inc. at the Young Women's Leadership School of Astoria. I want to talk briefly about what it takes for a family to walk their daughter through the doors of our programs. So many of our families are working families and in 2026, for working parents to hand their child to a program every afternoon, trusting their child's well-being, takes enormous courage. Yet these families choose us. That choice is built on trust and that trust took 12 years to establish.

Here is what trust produces inside a relationship: a girl can count on us. Our girls stop bracing and start growing. They show up as themselves. They take risks in front of people who already believe in them. That is where a young woman thrives — not in a program randomly assigned to her, but in a room where she was already known and seen as her greatest self. Developing young women is what all of us want for this City.

Girls Inc. at Astoria has built a competent and compassionate program. Our competence is in the numbers. This year alone we served 129 girls against a contract of 90 and every year we have surpassed the rigorous rubric for program performance. The compassion is a safe program with familiar faces that a working family can trust and hold accountable. It is unfair and unjust to assign these young women to a new provider chosen randomly through a non-collaborative process. A new provider only inherits a raw number served. It does not inherit 12 years of trust. That trust starts over at zero on the backs of girls and families who need it the most.

Right now I am quoting one of our sixth grade families who said: "Girls Inc. has been an invaluable partner in supporting the students at the Young Women's Leadership School of Astoria by providing a safe, empowering and enriching after-school environment that aligns to the school's mission of developing confident, college and career ready young women. Their programming offers students meaningful academic support, leadership development, mentorship and social-emotional learning opportunities that are specifically tailored to the needs of girls. Families benefit from reliable, high quality after-school care while students gain access to positive role models and programs that foster self-esteem, independence and community engagement. Continuing Girls Inc. as our after-school provider ensures that our students will continue to receive consistent, mission-driven support that strengthens both their academic success and personal growth." Thank you.

(00:15:44)

Thank you. My name is Guy Eric. I am a school counselor of over eight years at the Young Women's Leadership School of Astoria. I am here sharing my great concern at the potential loss of our long-standing after-school partner Girls Inc. and making a plea to retain this invaluable, ideally matched resource for our staff and students from all over Queens.

As a former after-school educator myself, I still serve as a citywide consultant for nonprofits on the social-emotional stakes of these kinds of programmatic collaborations. Now as a solo parent I have never been more convinced that the key to working family life is quality after-school in symbiosis with the school day. This means safe pick-up protocols, specialized extracurriculars that parents cannot afford elsewhere, skilled staff able to handle all the responsibility of the school day with all the enrichment that the kids need in those hours after their demanding classes.

Girls Inc. is the exemplar. Faces light up to see the staffers when they come into the building. There is a consistent level of service documented by their highest possible ratings. That was not centered in the procurement process. Their staff has our trust — in training that cannot be transferred to another provider, emergency protocols, community events, materials and a complex anecdotal system so an incident never lingers into a Snapchat battle at night. They are part of our students' reasons for attendance and economic growth. Their girls and teens have gone on to be interns. Their staffers have gone on to be our teachers in this beautiful sisterhood continuum.

These are not line-item benefits — they are intentional partnerships in our feminist corner of Queens. After-school is school and reassigning our girls and families is like taking a horn off our unicorn, or in the words of our students, cringe and low-key messed up. Our Girls Inc. team every day at 2:35 quiets a dysregulated room of kids to direct their attention to the task at hand. Bring it back, bring it back, bring it back. On behalf of our students, we hope you understood the assignment.

(00:19:59)

Good morning. My name is Priscilla Castro and I am the Paraprofessional Chapter Chair, representing 26,000 paraprofessionals across New York City. I am here today because for the past year and a half we have been loud and clear in regards to the Paraprofessional Respect Act and we want to ensure that it becomes a reality. Every year we spend $1.5 billion on contracts. Why? Because our students are not being served — they need the paraprofessionals. We cannot afford to lose our paraprofessionals. So I am here today to ask this body to pass the legislation in regards to the Paraprofessional Respect Act.

(00:20:56)

Thank you. Good morning, everybody. My name is John Camps. I am First Vice Chair of the Paraprofessional chapter. I want to thank all the Council members for their support this past year and for helping us get to this point. I hope that we can continue moving this forward and get it past the finish line. This legislation represents a critical step towards addressing longstanding inequities affecting paraprofessionals, who are among the most essential yet undercompensated members of our school communities. Every day, paraprofessionals provide indispensable support to students, particularly those with disabilities and complex needs, helping to ensure safe, inclusive and effective learning environments. The Respect Check would provide meaningful financial relief while also helping to stabilize a workforce that schools depend on. By improving compensation, this legislation will strengthen recruitment and retention efforts at a time when staffing shortages are impacting classrooms across the City. Equally important, this Bill sends a clear message that New York City values the contributions of paraprofessionals, recognizing that their work is not only a matter of fairness. It is an investment in the quality of education and support that our students receive. I respectfully urge you to support the passage of this Respect Check legislation and to advocate for policies that promote equity, stability and respect for all members of our school communities. Thank you all for your time.

(00:24:36)

Good morning, everyone. I am Karen Alford, Vice President for Non-Pedagogical Services at the UFT. I am going to talk to you today about four programs. First, United Community Schools: 32 schools across five boroughs, touching the lives of over 20,000 students and families. An investment in United Community Schools is an investment in educators, support, a focus on teaching and learning, as well as integrated supports for families. In 2025, support from the City Council helped United Community Schools feed over 71,000 families and provide over 48,000 health and wellness exams, screenings and check-ins. These supports translate into increased test scores, better attendance and student engagement at our schools. Teachers also feel the impact of working in a United Community Schools school, and we often hear our teachers say they feel more supported, secure and engaged. We know that retention right now is extremely important. Next, child care: investing in our youngest learners is a win for the City of New York, for our parents, our members and definitely our students. We are eager to partner with the Mayor and City Council to fulfill the promises of universal pre-K through 3-K and to ensure the successful rollout of both programs. We also want to make sure that providers are paid in a timely manner without delays.

We have to ensure that independent, non-network-affiliated home-based providers are included in this rollout and that our youngest learners are provided with the appropriate special education services when needed. The third program, BRAVE, is a hotline built to prevent bullying across the City. Our students are able to call in, and one phone call helps to protect our children and our families. Lastly, Dial-A-Teacher is a homework helpline. We have answered calls from tens of thousands of students. We operate Monday through Thursday in many languages, and with your support we are hoping to continue and expand all of those programs. Thank you.

(00:27:09)

Good morning. My name is Mary Vaccaro. I am the UFT Vice President for Education and the Director of the UFT Teacher Center. Thank you

(00:27:17)

for the opportunity to speak

(00:27:18)

about several initiatives that are making meaningful impact on educators, students and school communities across New York City. I would like to begin with the 46-year-old program, the UFT Teacher Center, which promotes teacher excellence and academic achievement for all students through high-quality professional development tailored to the unique needs of each school. The Teacher Center's work is grounded in the belief that when educators are supported, students thrive. The Teacher Center provided more than 126,000 hours of professional development to educators across the City. These learning opportunities range from instructional coaching and curriculum support to leadership development and collaborative planning. The impact is clear: 99% of participating educators reported that the professional learning they received significantly improved their practice and their students' learning outcomes. We have expanded our reach by opening more new Teacher Center sites this school year.

(00:28:16)

We have well over 200 site coaches embedded directly in schools, working shoulder to shoulder with educators to strengthen instruction, address challenges in real time and support student success. These coaches help schools implement evidence-based practices and build sustainable systems that continue to develop educators and students long after the work ends. We know that the UFT Teacher Center, providing support in classrooms and directly in schools, is not only a valuable resource but is much more cost-effective than using consultants in New York City's schools for teacher training and support. The Teacher Center has also played a major role in supporting citywide initiatives such as New York City Reads and the Reading for All initiative. The UFT Teacher Center has teamed up with the Goldie Hawn Foundation to introduce MindUP to over 300 schools, and with Think Equal to support social-emotional learning in pre-K, and with Common Point for our wellness days. These partnerships have given us the ability to make connections and to extend our reach to educators. We also provide support to any school that is requesting it. I want to quickly highlight the MAP program, the Member Assistance Program, which 16 years ago was developed in order to support our teachers and any educator in New York City in response to any training that they need for mental health issues. 50,000 educators across New York City have already used the counseling services. Thank you for your time.

(00:44:19)

My name is John Hislop. I am the President of Queens Library Local 1321. Speaker Menin, Deputy Speaker, Libraries Committee Chair Doctor Williams, Finance Committee Chair and fellow City Council members, thank you for giving me this opportunity to testify on behalf of the over 840 Local 1321 Queens Library Guild members.

Local 1321 members are disappointed. In 2016, with great fanfare and many news articles, Mayor de Blasio and the City Council made their agreement to, quote, baseline fund across the three library systems. From the August 26, 2016 City Council press release, these are their words: "Working alongside the administration, they were able to secure $43 million in baseline funding, which was adopted through negotiations of the 2017 budget."

Mayor de Blasio's baseline did not stop him from proposing cuts in successive preliminary budgets. Mayor Adams continued this strategy and proposed cuts in his preliminary budget. Mayor Mamdani continued the decades-long library budget dance and proposed cuts in his preliminary budget. With great social media fanfare, he celebrated the tenth anniversary of Mayor de Blasio's baseline by announcing his proposed underwhelming baseline. As we have experienced, mayoral declarations are not guaranteed baseline funding.

Local 1321 is calling for our elected officials to guarantee baseline funding and legislate or amend the City Charter to allocate 0.5% of the City's budget to New York City's public libraries, which would be an amount that provides substantial financing for library systems with a predictable and adequate funding source. Libraries can plan for the future, hire additional staff, provide sustainable six-day service, modernize the infrastructure, maintain clean branches, provide robust IT and offer additional programs and services.

Furthermore, having a guaranteed baseline will prevent unnecessary conflicts that Local 1321 and the library administration are having. Currently, the administration refuses to recognize and pay a vital group of public library service workers for their invaluable work. Mayor Mamdani, this country and our City needs you to succeed. That success comes with a strong foundation of public institutions, including libraries. Good morning.

(00:46:24)

Finance Chair Linda Lee, distinguished members of the Council. I am Donald, Executive Vice President for Local 372. I am here representing 24,000 New York City public school employees whose service supports 946,000 plus New York City students. Many of our members work in the communities that they live in and are neighbors to their students, their families and some of the parents of the school children themselves.

Local 372 represents again 2,600 school crossing guards who provide protection and supervision. The NYPD and the need for increased protection of student pedestrians and members of the communities make it essential for New York City to allocate funding for a higher number of school crossing guards. Local 372 respectfully requests that the restoration of 100 open positions are made for school crossing guards.

School lunch employees work hard every day, both in good times and in essential times, to serve breakfast and lunch to the community. They are required to do so according to federal nutrition standards and guidelines. This is the mandate they work under, serving every day. Local 372 respectfully requests funding towards an additional 1,000 school lunch employees.

An additional challenge that school lunch employees face is the condition of and proper ventilation in the kitchen spaces. Many of the kitchens that they work in every day have little to no cooling or ventilation and can reach 130 degrees. Proper air conditioning would filter out heat fumes and anything else that is in the air that can be harmful.

Since 1971, SAPIS workers have provided a range of mental health and intervention services in the largest school district in the nation. Local 372 has testified before this panel about the devastating impact of not having SAPIS within schools, the rising student reports of bullying at a time when social challenges to our students continue as well. Local 372 requests that the City of New York and the City Council maintain the dollar-for-dollar match for SAPIS within the state legislative budget on school support staff.

In conclusion, support staff are critical to a functioning school system. They create the space where teachers can teach and students can learn. Local 372 values these services and their contribution to our school system. Local 372 expresses its gratitude to the City Council for your support for our titles. We recognize that there are not enough resources to address every worthy cause or issue throughout the City and that tough decisions have to be made, but we thank you for your continued support. Good morning.

Good morning, Speaker Menin, Finance Chair, members of the City Council. My name is Vincent De Realia and I am a Lieutenant and President of the Uniformed Officers Union, Local 3621. I represent over 600 sergeants and captains of the New York City Fire Department. I have been with the Health and Hospitals Corporation and then the New York City Fire Department for 31 years.

I am here once again to report that New York City's EMS workforce is in crisis. The same officers, paramedics and EMTs who ushered the City through COVID, rushed through the burning towers on 9/11 and who save the lives of New Yorkers multiple times a day every day are being driven out of the ranks due to outrageously low and unfair pay and benefits. We have members living far outside the City because they cannot afford to live in the City they serve. Some are actually living in homeless shelters and in cars because they cannot afford the rent. It is an outrage and a slap in the face to City workers who serve all of us every day.

It does not go unnoticed that this is one of the most diverse uniform services in the City, consisting of predominantly non-white and largely female members. The lack of resources and inadequate pay for members jeopardizes the health and safety of every New Yorker. EMS operations represent about 80% of the overall 911 call volume. However, it only receives 25% of the FDNY budget and receives only 16% of the overall budget.

At the same time, due to high turnover and attrition, we are losing officers, EMTs and paramedics at an astonishing rate. We are going to lose up to 1,500 members within the next year alone and are staring down the barrel of a stunning 70% turnover rate within the next three to five years. EMTs and paramedics simply cannot afford to remain and feed their families. This is increasing response times to 911 calls for life-threatening emergencies.

The 2026 Mayor's Preliminary Management Report just found that in the first four months of fiscal year 2026, response times for life-threatening emergencies have increased to 12 minutes and 30 seconds. That is up two minutes and 30 seconds. That is an extra two and a half minutes while your spouse is having a stroke, your parents and grandparents fall and suffer from a serious injury, two and a half extra minutes to wait while you are suffering a massive heart attack, two and a half extra minutes to wait when someone calls about a person on the street or subway having a severe mental or emotional crisis. We have a system that is completely out of balance and teetering on the brink of collapse. Without change, it simply cannot keep up. New Yorkers must know that this is now more deadly in our City. If you or a loved one suffers a heart attack, life-threatening illness or serious injury, emergency health care in our City is in serious jeopardy. The survival rate for cardiac arrest is currently at its lowest, at 17%.

(00:52:22)

This is a public safety crisis. It did not happen overnight. It has been a long time in the making, with years of neglect and bad decisions. This Mayor did not cause it. Neither did this Council. In fact, this Council has been an advocate over the years and we want to thank you for your effort and help. We know that this Mayor is committed to equity, affordability and fairness. Pay equity is an affordability issue. Emergency medical care for New Yorkers must be a priority.

We ask you to help us do what is best for our City and prioritize this issue because it is literally a matter of life and death. We need to adequately compensate and resource our first responders so they can properly protect and save the people of this City during emergencies. Thank you very much.

(00:53:10)

Good morning. Thank you, Chair, and to all the Council members. My name is Michael Greco. I am the President of Local 2507, which represents the EMTs, paramedics and fire inspectors of the FDNY. For years, New York City has relied upon EMTs and paramedics to serve as the front line of the health care system, responding to over a million emergency medical calls annually. They arrive at shootings, cardiac arrests, overdoses, traumatic injuries and countless other life-threatening situations. Yet despite their critical role, they continue to be compensated at a level that bears little resemblance to either the cost of living in New York City or the immense responsibilities they carry. EMT starting salaries remain around $39,000 with top pay for officers still far too low.

This City has long acknowledged a recruitment and retention crisis and yet the budget fails to address the root cause: compensation. New York City EMTs and paramedics routinely leave to seek a livable wage somewhere else. The result is predictable: vacancies grow, overtime increases, burnout accelerates and experienced providers walk away from a profession they once loved. The consequences are clear. Response times for life-threatening emergencies have increased, with average response times approaching over 10 minutes and getting worse, driven in large part by staffing shortages and increasing call volume. The FDNY's own performance goals remain out of reach, not to mention the fact that every day we are holding jobs, sometimes for hours. Every minute matters during a cardiac arrest.

(00:55:03)

Every minute matters when a child cannot breathe or when a trauma patient is bleeding to death. Delayed ambulance responses are not merely operational statistics. They represent New Yorkers waiting longer for life-saving care. When elected officials choose not to invest in staffing and compensation, they are making a policy choice that directly impacts emergency response capabilities and public safety outcomes.

The City budget reflects its priorities and once again it fails to make a substantial commitment towards solving one of the most obvious public safety challenges facing New York City. The workforce shortage is no mystery. The solution is not complicated. Competitive compensation attracts qualified candidates, retains experienced providers, reduces turnover, improves staffing levels and ultimately decreases response times. New Yorkers deserve ambulances that arrive quickly when their loved ones are experiencing the worst moments of their lives. They deserve a government willing to invest in the professionals who provide that care.

Until the City commits to meaningful pay parity and long-term workforce investment, response times will continue to suffer, staffing shortages will continue to worsen and public safety will continue to pay the price. The question is no longer whether New York City can afford to invest. The question is how much longer can New Yorkers afford the City not to. I just want to add that in the past 10 years New York City has hired about 6,000 EMS workers. We are currently 2,000 under staffing levels. That is an investment of a quarter billion dollars in training that has not gotten them over the 4,000-person hump. That is the staffing crisis we are talking about here today.

The people doing this work — counselors, social workers, teachers, principals — simply did not have the tools they needed. The information to help a student was scattered across systems that did not talk to each other. So with input from thousands of educators we built the tool we all wished we had when we worked in schools. Today over 18,000 staff across the system use this software platform to plan for students, catch the ones falling off track before it is too late, and coordinate with people supporting them, all in a single real-time view. It is how districts manage City initiatives and it is the only mechanism connecting school data to community shelters and the hundreds of community providers our most vulnerable students rely on.

Cutting this investment or reducing it by a few million also threatens more than $5 million in annual philanthropic investment that extends every City dollar, and it would derail the development roadmap built with scores of partners across City agencies and CBOs. There is no cheaper path — for-profit vendors would charge many times more than this contract for the same scope, and as we all know, the DOE has no precedent for building software this complex in-house. (02:13:14)

The challenge that I faced as a principal, and the challenge that administrators, teachers and counselors face across the system, is not that the data exists somewhere but that the data exists in too many different places, and that any time anyone wants to do anything with that data, the system reverts back to practices from 20 years ago of downloading spreadsheets onto local machines where the data still lives, where the plans live in multiple places, where it is impossible to collaborate across organizations. That is the problem that we have solved with the portal.

It is not just a one-way reporting tool, as are the tools that are presented as alternatives. It is a complex case management, project management, initiative management and student planning tool. Administrators, counselors and teachers can enter plans into the portal. They can create groupings for reading groups, for after-school programs, for internships, and then they can track and collaborate to implement those programs and to understand how students are doing against the goals of attendance, graduation and outcomes.

We urge you to create and fund the Journalism for All citywide initiative in the FY27 budget. Journalism for All is not a short-term solution or quick fix. It is a tested wraparound model for total support designed to scale across the city while still ensuring support meets schools where they are, instead of forcing them into a one-size-fits-all model. Currently, very few schools offer any sort of journalism education, as shown by Local Law 27, which the Council passed last year thanks to CM Joseph. It also showed that access is starkly divided along the same equity gaps that plague our school system. We have already seen the impact of Journalism for All in our pilot cohort in schools like many of the students speaking today, and I am so excited to introduce you to our student leaders who can tell you firsthand about the impacts of access, or lack thereof. Thank you for your time.

While many teenagers today are struggling with real life social interaction in this increasingly online world, many teenage discovery guides have been developing in the opposite direction.

So the bulk of the WCS's local operating funds come from outside. This means that programs like the discovery guide program really do live and die on the whims of government spending. Pratt Center has a long history of researching strategies for affordable and equitable planning and housing in New York City. Our report on community land trusts, "Gaining Ground," identified an essential tool for advancing neighborhood affordability and fighting displacement, particularly in historically low- and moderate-income communities of color. The report identified policy recommendations to scale and sustain the initiative, including the three we call for here today.

Increasing funding for the City Council has been instrumental to the CLT initiative. Addressing contract delays and providing funding for the initiative at $3 million for fiscal year 2027, up from $1.5 million in fiscal year 2026, will provide essential support to 23 organizations, including 19 active CLT assistance organizations including ours, up from 15 groups last year. Enhanced funding for the citywide initiative will enable Pratt Center to expand our data analysis, mapping, community engagement, research, planning and technical assistance services across the City. Pratt Center has not previously been funded through the citywide initiative, but as members of NYCCLI we have provided assistance on multiple related projects each year, ranging from research and mapping projects to longer-term community engagement and

(03:18:22)

initiatives. This past year, for example, we partnered with the Real Edge and Mira CLT to design a facilitated community engagement to identify community priorities for commercial and community spaces on lots held by the CLT. In addition to individual projects, we develop and provide educational materials and programs on City land use policies such as City of Yes and land use ballot measures, and help groups understand what the impact will be for their organizations and communities. We would be able to expand these technical services to more CLTs across the City with additional funding.

We also urge counsel to support the creation of a $50 million CLT acquisition fund. Mayor Mamdani's campaign platform included the creation of such a fund, which is essential to enable seizing opportunities to bring land and housing into permanently affordable community control.

We have 15 buildings spread out over 21 acres and we are home to more than 5,000 New Yorkers, making us one of the largest affordable cooperatives in New York City. At a time when there is a housing crisis, we are very proud to be part of the City's housing solution, but maintaining that affordability is increasingly difficult.

Built in 1962, Penn South requires significant capital improvements to ensure that our residents, the majority of which are senior citizens, are living in homes that are safe. Consistently over the next several years, we will begin our largest and most expensive capital projects: underground utility piping, boilers, chillers, roofs, elevators and facade work. We have found out that because of a technicality, we will not be eligible for J-51, something we have relied on historically to be able to serve the needs of the residents affordably. At this time when we are already struggling financially, we now have to be able to comply with Local Law 97.

Just to be clear, make no mistake: we are fully supportive of the City's climate goals and we want to be able to meet the challenges of Local Law 97. However, with the amount of work that needs to be done on an infrastructure level, including the electrification of a power plant and other improvements, we are saying to you that we can only accomplish this through partnership with the City. Please, we are willing and able to come to the table to find a solution. Thank you.

The Boston Secor explosion is a cautionary tale of what project-based Section 8 leads to: displacement, injury and death. Right now, the City has plans to have $15 billion go towards fueling project-based Section 8 conversions. One such attempt being the demolition of the Fulton and Elliot-Chelsea Houses in Chelsea. This demolition is guaranteed to displace the current residents and create luxury towers most New Yorkers will never afford.

We must not create or bankroll any more disastrous project-based Section 8 housing. I, along with my comrades in the Young Communist League, am calling on the City Council to reallocate the budget towards comprehensive modernization of Section 9, stop handing over public housing to greedy companies that would rather see tenants displaced or dead than taken care of, prevent the demolition of Section 9 housing like Fulton-Elliot Chelsea, and keep public housing public. Thank you, Chair and members of the Committee. My name is Megan French-Marcelin and I am testifying on behalf of the New York City Alternatives to Incarceration and Reentry Coalition. We are a network of 12 organizations that provide community-based services to justice-involved New Yorkers. In fiscal year 2025, our organizations served more than 69,000 New York City residents.

We appreciate the administration's restoration of funding for alternatives to incarceration and reentry services in the executive budget, including increases respectively. These investments reflect a commitment to the evidence-based public safety strategies that reduce reliance on incarceration and strengthen communities. However, additional investment is needed and the fiscal case is clear. Rikers Island costs over $500,000 per person per year. Meanwhile, a recent analysis of the programs in our coalition showed that we generate more than $60 million in jail savings each year and more than $70 million in state prison costs every year.

Put another way, for every dollar invested in alternatives to incarceration, the City realizes approximately $4.70 in savings. We therefore are urging the administration to add an additional $10 million to alternatives to incarceration and reentry services respectively, and the City Council to build on the administration's commitment by providing additional discretionary funding to the coalition through the alternatives to incarceration and reentry programs initiatives so that we can scale these programs and serve New Yorkers in communities better. Thank you.

(03:50:06)

Good afternoon, Committee members, and thank you for the opportunity to speak here today. My name is Jason Pauline, Chief Program Officer at EXALT. EXALT serves justice-impacted youth aged 15 to 19, many of whom are chronically disengaged from their schools, over-age and under-credited, and reading at a fourth-grade level. Our powerful model is comprised of tangible skill development classes and paid internship experiences necessary to not only avoid recidivism, but to truly experience the best that life has to offer, because that is what our young people deserve.

Utilizing this cross-sector approach, EXALT has achieved remarkable results with young people that in many ways society at large has not. We are proud to say that 75% of the young people that come to us with open court cases have their sentences reduced or dismissed, 98% are on track to graduate by age 20, and even two years after graduating from our program, 95% do not recidivate. This is a huge savings to our City and State as the estimated cost per year to incarcerate a young person ranges from $266,000 to as high as $892,000. EXALT's model is conservatively 18 to as much as 60 times less expensive than incarceration, providing government billions in savings.

We applaud the restoration of funding for alternatives to incarceration programs and reentry services in the executive budget, including increases respectively. These investments represent an important commitment to community-based public safety strategies that reduce reliance on incarceration and support New Yorkers in building stable and productive lives. At the same time, additional investment is needed to bring these programs to scale. A recent fiscal impact analysis conducted by the Data Collaborative for Justice found that the coalition's services have generated more than $60 million in savings for New York City by reducing reliance on incarceration at Rikers Island, demonstrating that investments in community-based alternatives produce both fiscal and public safety benefits. We urge the City Council to build on the administration's commitment by providing additional discretionary funding through the alternatives to incarceration and reentry programs. This investment would enable our 12 coalition organizations to serve more New Yorkers and further expand access to effective community-based alternatives that promote safety, stability and opportunity.

Good afternoon, Chair and members of the Committee on Finance. My name is Gloria Kim. I am the Director of Policy at the Human Services Council. We are a membership organization representing 180 human services providers in New York. I want to draw your attention to the fact that 28 Council Members have signed on, which demonstrates a clear commitment to addressing the long-standing wage inequities faced by human services workers. These workers carried our City through the pandemic and continue to serve communities under difficult circumstances. Yet many remain significantly underpaid compared to their public sector counterparts. They deserve fair compensation for the critical work they do.

We also appreciate the implementation of the final year of the human services cost-of-living adjustment. However, we urge the City to establish a multiyear COLA structure moving forward. Predictable and recurring adjustments would help providers retain staff, manage rising costs and ensure continuity of care for New Yorkers. At the same time, these investments cannot achieve their full impact unless the City addresses its contracting crisis. Too many nonprofits continue operating programs for months, sometimes more than a year, before contracts are registered and payments are received. Providers are forced to cover payroll, rent and program expenses while waiting for reimbursement, placing enormous strain on already stretched organizations. Nonprofits need a system that treats them as essential partners, not organizations trapped in administrative delays. We thank you for your continued leadership and commitment to strengthening both the human services workforce and the communities they serve.

Very much. Good afternoon. My name is Adama Bah. I am the Executive Director and Founder of Africana. New York City spends thousands of dollars on language line services, yet despite this investment, many Black immigrants still cannot access services in the language they understand. We routinely see Pulaar speakers offered French interpretation, Soninke speakers expected to communicate in English, and Wolof and Bambara speakers unable to find interpretation at all. Language access is not simply about translating words. It is about ensuring that people can understand their rights, access healthcare, support their children in school, secure housing and find employment with dignity.

The current approach is not working. It is easy to provide French because it is viewed as a convenient solution, but French is not the first language of many African immigrants. A person who speaks Pulaar is not automatically fluent in French. I understand that public funding cannot be awarded based on race. However, the City can invest in organizations with demonstrated expertise serving communities that speak Pulaar, Wolof, Bambara and other underrepresented African languages. These organizations already have the trust relationships and cultural knowledge necessary to reach communities that government systems continue to miss.

We also need dedicated workforce development funding. Too often, immigrant workforce programs are designed around communities that already have significant infrastructure and language support, while Black immigrant communities remain underserved. We need workforce training, licensing assistance, job placement and career pathways that are accessible in the languages our communities actually speak. I also want to address halal food access. Halal is not an ethnicity. Halal is a religious requirement. Too often, conversations about halal food contracts erase the presence of Black Muslims, despite the fact that Black Muslims have been part of New York City for generations. Black Muslims include African Americans whose families have been here for centuries as well. NICSI is a coalition of five established immigrant-serving and refugee resettlement organizations: Catholic Charities Community Services, Church World Service, CAMBA, CommonPoint and the International Rescue Committee. The coalition is collectively seeking $5 million, $1 million for each of the five coalition members, to respond to the urgent and evolving needs of at least 4,000 immigrant New Yorkers. NICSI is designed to bring resettlement-informed case management and other services not just to refugees, but to all immigrants: asylum seekers, humanitarian parolees, undocumented immigrants and mixed-status families. It includes populations that are often unable to access resettlement programs and public benefits.

NICSI focuses on meeting urgent stabilization needs while also supporting integration and self-sufficiency through comprehensive assessment, cultural orientation, family strengthening, individualized economic mobility planning and formal employment services for those authorized to work. We commend the City Council for supporting immigration legal services at a time of unprecedented changes in immigration policies and increased enforcement, and want to highlight the importance of wraparound social services that will allow immigrants to focus on their legal challenges while NICSI addresses their basic needs, such as access to food, housing, medical care and other important needs. It is a critical and opportune time to support the NICSI initiative while the coalition member organizations still have highly experienced, linguistically and culturally competent staff, many of whom have lived the immigrant and refugee experience like myself. In light of massive federal funding cuts, without NICSI, this expertise and these resources will be lost.

For many residents, especially in communities with limited access to parks and open space, this investment will create safer connections to nature, recreation and local businesses.

Good afternoon, Chairs and members of the committee. My name is Mateen Serc and I serve as co-chair of the Housing, Land Use and Zoning Committee from Manhattan Community Board 19. Public housing exists because the private sector will never profit from housing low-income families. Its true purpose is to solve the challenge of housing, not to recycle failed private sector delusions. Instead of gifting billions to private investors, this Council must invest directly in sustainable public funding sources and public social partnerships. We do not need top-down agency road shows like "NYCHA in Your Neighborhood" that treat systemic failure as a public relations issue. Instead, NYCHA must expand its programmatic footprint across City agencies to close operational gaps. For example, graffiti remains on First Houses because the City excludes NYCHA from free removal services, and NYCHA lacks the landmark-specific experience to hire specialized vendors that could easily be supplied by the City.

While Mayor Mamdani's block-by-block housing plan injects critical capital into physical assets, budget mandates are quietly hollowing out the social safety net inside those exact same properties. Physical modernization is not a victory if the daycares, senior centers and community hubs built into these developments are starved of operational staff and resources. The block-by-block plan allocates $25,600,000 to turn over vacant units and evaluate offline spaces for child care. However, evaluation without immediate capital allocation is just paperwork. Many of these spaces require deep environmental remediation and severe structural repairs. Habitability does not stop at the apartment doorway. When a community space suffers a plumbing failure, an entire community loses essential services.

Finally, accountability and community trust requires a shift in strategy. The administration sounds the alarm on abysmal voter turnout with resident associations, yet proposes deploying central bureaucracy behind folding tables. I am urging this Council to allocate baseline funding to pilot a resident management corporation program, turning resident leadership into a permanent fixture of public housing stabilization. Thank you.

Chair and committee members, thank you for giving me the opportunity to testify today. My name is Rose Cobb. I live in Brooklyn with my husband and my two children. I am devastated by this decision and I wanted to come here today to tell you one reason why.

Power of Two provides many, many benefits to the families we serve, but I wanted to talk today about our young children. One in five mothers experiences some form of pregnancy-related depression or anxiety. Black and Latina mothers experience these mental health challenges at twice that rate. Since 2015, work with Power of Two has been demonstrated to eliminate depressive symptoms in 64% of the caregivers we serve — a result that is both meaningful and life-changing. A mother's mental health directly shapes her child's development. When a mother is struggling, her child is affected. Caregiver depression is associated with delays in language and cognitive development, increased behavioral problems and deficits in social and emotional skills that follow children into the classroom and beyond.

The health and well-being of mother and child are inseparable. When we invest in mothers, we invest in their children. At a time when parents and caregivers across the city are contending with unaffordable housing, reductions in federal food assistance, economic instability and growing stress on family systems, our program is often one of the few consistent, relationship-based supports available to families with very young children. Without Power of Two's preventative services, families who lose access will not be redirected to equivalent care — they will go without. At Power of Two, we can say with confidence and with evidence that prevention cuts will reverse the progress New York City has made to improve outcomes for New York City families. It is imperative that current prevention funding be maintained, whether through ACS or the Department of Health and Mental Hygiene, at $3.1 million and modestly expanded over the next several years as the administration and City Council continue shaping the City's long-term strategy. Thank you.

Good afternoon, Chair and members of the City Council. Thank you for the opportunity to testify today. My name is Amy Wilkerson. I am the Chief Program Officer at Forestdale Inc. Forestdale is a longstanding provider of preventive services in Queens. I am here with deep concern and urgency regarding the Administration for Children's Services' proposal to close our family support programming in North Queens. The decision came as a complete surprise. Based on prior communication from the Mayor's Office and Social Services leadership, we understood that critical social services would not be reduced in this budget. However, this action is not a technical adjustment. It is a direct service cut that will immediately impact at-risk families and weaken the preventive services system.

Forestdale is one of the highest performing preventive services providers in New York City. We ranked in the top tier for multiple years, including achieving the number one ranking for family support in both fiscal year 2024 and fiscal year 2025. The City has consistently relied on our work to stabilize families, prevent foster care placement and reduce long-term system involvement. The program slated for closure is not theoretical. It is a lifeline. It serves one of the highest-needs areas in Queens, second only to Jamaica, in child protective investigations and preventive cases. At this moment we are working intensively with more than 50 families — approximately 150 individuals — and annually we serve over 500 families facing crisis, such...

(05:42:14)

...as domestic violence, mental health challenges, housing instability and the risk of child removal. Forestdale's approach is unique in New York City. Our model is comprehensive, robust and community-based, designed not only to stabilize families in crisis but to strengthen them long term. Through this program, families receive parenting support, mental health and trauma-informed interventions, access to healthcare, cash and material assistance and connections to education and employment resources. This is not a narrow service. It is a coordinated safety net that surrounds families with the support they need to stay together.

Our results speak for themselves. 90% of families we serve avoid foster care placement. For many, we are the only support system they have. The $1.9 million cut will directly impact 14 staff, eliminating those positions, destabilizing our broader preventive services infrastructure and jeopardizing support for families across Brooklyn and Queens. We also have more than 20 skilled people who will be impacted by this as well — experts in trauma, domestic violence and family stability. To put it shortly...

(05:43:35)

...the families we serve cannot absorb this loss and the City cannot afford the consequences. Preventive services are what keeps families together and reduces future costs for security and crisis systems. We urge you to partner with us and prevent the closure of this program. The stakes are simply too high. Thank you for your time and your leadership.

(05:43:58)

Good afternoon, Chair and members of the Committee. Thank you for the opportunity to testify today. My name is Krista Larson and I am the Assistant Executive Director for Preventive Services at Forestdale. For many years, Forestdale has provided high-quality preventive services in Queens. Our family support program has consistently been one of the highest rated in New York City. Yet our North Queens program is among the programs ACS has proposed eliminating.

Preventive services work because they reach families early, before stress, instability and unmet needs escalate into deeper crisis or family separation. It is no small thing to invite someone into your home and share the most personal details of your family's life. Families make that choice when they believe the support will be respectful, effective and grounded in partnership. They do it because they want to protect their children and move through a difficult period with help that builds on their strengths. Families consistently tell us that their relationship with preventive staff is one of the most important parts of the service.

Many of our families experience tremendous isolation. They may be new to New York City, new to the US, or may be separated from their support systems because of ruptured relationships. Families tell us that the staff provide a combination of information, encouragement, a listening ear and some straight talk. It is these relationships that help families believe that change is possible. In preventive services, the relationship is not separate from the work — it is what makes the work possible.

When a preventive services contract is cut, families experience the loss immediately. They lose continuity, stability and the momentum that comes from steady, relationship-based support. Families lose not only access to services but also the trusted connection that helps build confidence, progress and hope. Starting over with a new provider means retelling painful experiences, rebuilding trust from the beginning and trying to engage all over again at the very moment consistency matters most.

I urge the Council to restore funding for this contract and protect continuity in preventive services for families in Queens. Thank you.

Good afternoon. Thank you, Chair Lee and members of the Council for the opportunity to testify today. My name is Erica Mitchell. Like many of our families of color in our City, we were told never to trust the system, so we stayed in the shadows and did not get help. I became a social worker to break that cycle.

For nearly 50 years, CAMBA has been doing exactly that — building an unshakable bridge of trust in communities where the system has historically caused harm. Today, CAMBA serves nearly 80,000 New Yorkers annually across 180 programs and for 30 years has served in preventive services in Brooklyn. I am here to convey the devastating impact of terminating CAMBA's primary prevention services in Brownsville and Bushwick. In these neighborhoods, systemic poverty, homelessness and community violence do not exist in silos — they go hand in hand. When families are displaced by housing instability, the stress fractures the home. When a community is impacted by gun violence, trauma ripples through every household. True prevention means recognizing that you cannot address a child's safety without addressing the environment the child lives in.

To understand the human cost of these cuts, let me share Evan's story. On February 20th, a devastating fire destroyed her home and her family lost everything, including their beloved cat, Milo. In a moment of absolute trauma, our prevention programs mobilized immediately to step into the ashes and secure clothing, emergency supplies and housing stability. If you eliminate this contract, you disrupt the fragile trust of countless families who finally felt safe enough to let a provider into their lives.

In fiscal year 2025, Brooklyn recorded more than 9,000 child welfare reports, with over 2,100 concentrated right there in CM East Brooklyn. Pulling our team out of high-crisis communities leaves vulnerable communities exposed to systemic issues of homelessness, gentrification and economic displacement. Today, over a dozen full-time staff members are trapped in an agonizing professional limbo without a clear timeline from the City. These frontline professionals are being asked to absorb the trauma of their community while facing total uncertainty regarding their own jobs, healthcare and families.

Defunding preventive programs does not save municipal dollars. It simply exchanges proactive, home-based healing for the far more costly and traumatic downstream price of foster care and family separation. We need a solution that ensures our families and our workforce that serves them are protected. We urge the Council to partner with us to keep families safe in New York City. Thank you.

Good afternoon, Chair Lee and members of the Committee. My name is Gladstone Johnson. I serve as the Executive Director of Bronx Neighborhood Housing Services. Thank you for the opportunity to speak. New York City currently has more than 7,000 one-to-four bedroom properties, and over 9,000 in my area in the Bronx, currently at risk of entering the tax lien sale process. These numbers reflect not just properties but families, generational assets and the backbone of many of our neighborhoods. Without timely intervention, manageable maintenance and financial challenges can quickly escalate into housing instability, foreclosure risk and eventual displacement. Too often homeowners come to us only after a manageable issue has become a full-blown crisis. What begins as a leaky roof, broken light, overgrown property or deferred maintenance can rapidly lead to code violations, mounting repair costs, insurance compliance issues and significant debt, including property tax and water arrears. Senior homeowners and those living with disabilities are especially vulnerable, often lacking the resources or capacity to address these issues before they spiral.

This pilot program would enable trusted, community-based organizations like ours to address these issues by providing proactive property management support. We can help homeowners maintain compliance, address repairs before they worsen and navigate financial challenges before they lead to tax lien sales. Thank you for allowing us to speak today.

I am Rick Karen, Executive Director of an all-volunteer nonprofit advocacy group whose mission is the reactivation of the Rockaway Beach Branch, Queens' only north-south rail corridor. Queens needs both transit and parks, but they must be designed together or one will get in the way of the other.

We are asking that the $43 million for the Met Hub project be rescinded, as it is part of a $500 million unfunded Queensway park plan. It is needless. Construction will make reactivation less likely and cost the City and its residents billions in lost economic and environmental benefits.

We agree that the segments valued as parks, which make up 33 of the 47 acres of Queens Link, are necessary, but you need transit in order to get there. The five-acre Met Hub park is not in a park-poor area but is actually right next to the underutilized and hard-to-access 540-acre Forest Park.

The City never bothered to study how this irreplaceable land should be used to deliver the greatest value to the most residents, nor did it ask the residents what they wanted. Queens Link did, and the answer is that transit and parks will deliver the best value. The residents, by a 3-to-1 margin, preferred transit over parks. But the good news is Queens deserves both and can have both.

The warning is that building a park on the rail corridor would pose a challenge to reactivation, but the City went ahead anyway and, contrary to its promises, designed a park that Mayor Mamdani acknowledged would have to be torn up to add transit. Building anything on this transit right-of-way in the middle of a transit desert is a bad idea at any cost.

The Rockaway Beach Branch is not like the High Line, which faced the wrecking ball, but a viable rail corridor in the middle of a densely populated, transit-poor area — the subject of a $500,000 U.S. DOT Queens study that will start this fall. The MTA announced that it will evaluate reactivation as part of its 20-year needs assessment in two years. No city that is serious about improving public transit and restoring the rail service that it took away 64 years ago would build a new park on that same corridor. Doing so is equivalent to cutting Queens in half all over again. I do not know about you, but that is not the kind of city I want to live in.

In conclusion, we ask the Mayor and the City Council to remove Queensway funding from the current budget and work together with the State to ensure that Queens Link's integrated subway and parks proposal is included in the 2032-2034 capital plan. Thank you very much.

(06:05:16)

To the members of the Council and the Finance Committee, my name is Matthew Mown. I have lived here my entire life and currently reside in Middle Village. I would like to tell a story.

In the 1960s, a large rail line was abandoned due to a decrease in business. A local grassroots organization rose up and submitted a proposition to the Council to revitalize the corridor and bring transit and trails back to the area, serving hundreds of thousands of people in what is otherwise a transit desert. Now, to some of you this may sound like a project in New York City called Queens Link, but in actuality it is describing the current Beltline project in Atlanta. Even though the Beltline project was initiated 27 years ago, not a cent was spent, a train built or an inch of track revitalized — why? The city decided to build the park first. This story serves as a cautionary tale for the current Council.

Currently, $43 million is allocated in the Mayor's Executive Budget for Queensway, a project that would redesignate the existing rail line in Queens as parkland. I am here to warn not only the Council but also those around us that this is a terrible idea. According to New York's interpretation of the public trust doctrine, once a park is built, it becomes increasingly impossible, if not incredibly rare, to do any further development. Any transit addition is delayed and debated indefinitely until effectively becoming canceled. We need more than promises from the Council or the Mayor to eventually integrate rail, because as history has shown us, it does not work this way.

Luckily, a proposed alternative to Queensway called Queens Link has fully laid-out plans to revitalize the existing rail and integrate 33 new acres of parkland — the best of both worlds. But it is being ignored by the Mayor and the Council.

Now, I live in Middle Village but I go to college in downtown Brooklyn. The only reasonable way to get there is by taking the Q53 down the crowded Woodhaven Boulevard. Let me tell you, there are 10,000 riders on that bus weekly. That is 10,000 more weekly riders than the least-used subway line. The rail is right there. Why are we not using it?

This last year was the first time I ever voted in a local election and I am proud to have voted for my representative Phil Wong and Mayor Mamdani. But right now it feels as if you are turning your back on the community and depriving transit from the people who need it most. I am urging the Council to learn from the mistakes of their colleagues in Atlanta, take time and look into the possibility of Queens Link, and ask the Mayor to cut Queensway from the Executive Budget. Otherwise you turn your back on the people you promised to improve the City for. Do not make this mistake.

(06:07:41)

Thank you, Chair and members of the Finance Committee. My name is Mike Scala and I serve in a volunteer capacity as counsel and legal adviser to Queens Link. I am speaking today in strong support of Queens Link, which would bring both new subway service and new parkland to Queens by reactivating the former Rockaway Beach Branch, and against the current $43 million allocated in the budget for phase one of Queensway, also called the Met Hub project.

I was born and raised in South Queens. I went to high school at Bayside Cardozo. My commute to and from school took up two hours each way. While I was spending four hours in transit every day, my classmates were enjoying their lives. This ugly reality continues to this day, as a recent survey found that 16% of residents in my assembly district traveled 90 minutes or more each way to work. That means thousands of us are commuting over three hours every single day in the greatest city in the world. This is unacceptable.

Two competing concepts exist for the deactivated Rockaway Beach Branch right-of-way: the park-only Queensway and the dual...

(06:08:45)

...purpose Queens Link, which combines subway and park uses. Queensway would be built directly on the former rail bed, which would virtually eliminate the possibility of ever restoring transit there. The MTA itself recognized in its 2020-2023 20-year needs assessment that a linear park along the corridor would create a challenge for any future transit alternatives. Once a right-of-way is converted to other uses, it almost never returns to transit.

Queens Link would create the first north-south subway line in Queens, extending the M line south from Rego Park to Ozone Park and then over the A train tracks to the Rockaways, increasing service to the Rockaways by up to 265%. It would also let the G train run all the way to Forest Hills, increasing capacity on the crowded Queens Boulevard local lines by 50% and connecting central Queens with north Queens and Brooklyn. A study by the Steer Group found that Queens Link would remove millions of vehicle trips each year, making streets safer and lowering harmful emissions, while adding up to 33 acres of new parks and trails integrated with the subway design. Over 20 years, the study concluded the project would more than pay for itself by creating jobs, saving New Yorkers time and reducing health care costs tied to air pollution and traffic crashes.

So in conclusion, Queens needs parks and trains. Queens Link would do both. I urge the Committee to preserve this irreplaceable right-of-way for future transit and to support Queens Link. Thank you.

(06:10:16)

Good afternoon, Chair and committee members. My name is Jasper. I am a City employee and a volunteer for Queens Link. In the past four years I have spoken to thousands of Queens residents who overwhelmingly want the same thing: robust public transit and parks they can be proud of — the same amenities other boroughs take for granted. But we have been waiting so long for change that we are disillusioned by what is possible. Car dependency is stealing our health, our time and our money.

The Queensway's argument, now echoed by Mayor Mamdani, is that building something now is better than building nothing, and that building a park will not preclude rail reactivation. Atlanta proves otherwise. The Beltline was a proposed mixed-use light rail and trail project, but in 2004 the Trust for Public Land — the same national nonprofit fighting for the Queensway — recommended that approach. Over 20 years later, the Beltline's trails are almost complete and not a single track has been laid. The current mayor, Andre Dickens, just walked back support for a shovel-ready light rail component of the project, capitulating to developers and anti-transit dark money groups.

New York is facing the same threat. The chosen location for the first phase of Queensway overlaps entirely with future tracks and station infrastructure, according to their own feasibility study. Once a multimillion-dollar park is built, we will be left with no other alternative but to argue for ripping up the park to build the rail, sparking the same opposition we are watching unfold in Atlanta. Public Advocate Jumaane Williams called the Queensway fiscally foolish and irresponsible, and I could not agree more.

If the Mayor is as committed to expanding public transit as he says he is, then he should not be prioritizing aesthetics over solutions to the borough's immense transportation needs. My question for you is this: is this park worth denying South Queens residents, who already endure the longest commutes in the nation, the right to affordable and convenient mass transit?

Respectfully, I urge this City Council to not allocate any more funding to the Queensway project in this or any future City budget. Instead, I ask the Mayor and the Council to work with advocates and leadership at the State level to prioritize rails and trails in the next capital plan. Queens deserves nothing less. Thank you.

Good afternoon, Chair and members of the Committee on Finance. I am Noel Williams and I am currently a junior attending high school and a resident of Rosedale. Fundamentally, as this Council continues debating whether Queens Link is the right path forward, there are clear truths that I urge the Council to acknowledge in the course of this discussion.

First and foremost, Queens Link is currently projected at roughly 75,000 daily riders, primarily from those who formerly utilized congested bus routes such as the Q52 and Q53 Select Bus Services. Additionally, Queens Link's initial business case has shown that the combined decreases in transit times, quality of life improvements and reductions in congestion mean that for every dollar that goes to Queens Link, a dollar and ten cents is returned. Accounting for these factors, it is evident that Queens Link will serve to be a net positive upon the city as a whole, especially communities in the Rockaways and the Woodhaven neighborhood, that not only will see higher business traffic but will save money on time spent in daily congestion and traffic along the Woodhaven and Cross Bay Boulevards.

This is especially important to me as a student attending high school at which people routinely from my class complain about the overcrowding and congestion of the Q52, Q53 and Q54 Select Bus Services. Many of my friends from the Rockaways whose parents work in the region could gain significantly from the increased economic activity and less congested roads leading into the Rockaways. Taking the Q52 or Q53 bus services should not be an experience that makes me yearn for the days of pandemic-era social distancing.

Under the status quo there is no possibility for Queens Link to coexist with Queensway unless Queensway diverts off the abandoned rail right of way. We will lose out on a once-in-a-generation opportunity to deliver rail infrastructure to communities that need transit alternatives. To me and many southeast Queens residents, failure to seize on this opportunity and to deliver rail infrastructure is failure to advocate for hundreds of thousands of New Yorkers to be able to do the main thing that makes us New Yorkers: taking the train to school, work or wherever we need to be. I would argue it would be a greater misjudgment than any of the calls made during the last Knicks game, and that is not something that we need.

Okay, perfect. Good evening. Sorry, good evening. My name is Kevin Wolf. I am here on behalf of the Center for New York City Neighborhoods, and we are one of the largest homeowner service organizations in New York City.

(06:36:18)

She was on the brink of homelessness when she came to us for help. Miss B's story highlights the challenges many New Yorkers face in buying and keeping their homes. Since 2008, the Center for New York City Neighborhoods has promoted and protected affordable homeownership so that working and middle class New Yorkers can remain in strong and stable communities. We are a citywide network of housing counseling and legal services organizations. We have served 200,000 homeowners, and each year our network helps to save more than 2,000 homeowners from foreclosure and eviction.

It is time for New York to deliver for homeowners like Miss B, and we commend Mayor Mamdani for putting forward a housing plan that will do just that. Increasing home repair financing, wrapping up emergency funding through the MAP program, supporting basement legalization, deed theft prevention, property tax reform and improving access to tax exemptions for our seniors and disabled — we can help stabilize owner-occupied housing and preserve affordability. Homeowners are facing increasing challenges from rising foreclosure filings, which grew by 8% this year, to deed scams targeting vulnerable residents, to unmet home repair needs. That is why we are requesting that the City Council support our homeowner services.

These investments will strengthen our outreach, expand our legal services and housing counseling, and bring direct support to communities that need it most. With the Council's support, we can protect homeowners, preserve generational wealth and ensure New York City remains a place where working families can stay. Thank you very much for listening.

Thank you so much. All right. Good afternoon, Council Members. Thank you so much for sticking around and staying engaged. It has been a really enlightening hearing. My name is Paula Siegel. I am Senior Staff Attorney in Take Root Justice's Equitable Neighborhoods Unit, and I am here today representing the Abolish the Tax Lien Sale Coalition, which has organized and advocated since 2022 to abolish the current lien sale system and replace it with a just and equitable system of city debt collection. Take Root, where I work, is a founding member of the coalition.

I want to address two things in the executive budget. One is that it actually includes revenue from a tax lien sale, but it also provides no funding for outreach to owners and residents in lien sale eligible buildings. Neither of these items are acceptable and neither should go forward in the final budget at the end of the month. I encourage the Council to think about them separately.

The Council must pass a budget that includes $3 million in outreach funding for residents of lien sale eligible properties. Struggling homeowners will only see their city debt increase if they do not have the support they need to evaluate their options and find a pathway to staying in their homes. Whether or not those liens are about to be sold to a third party, those services are needed. Tenants in buildings with outstanding debt need to be informed of their rights and connected with groups that can help them improve their living conditions. They need that whether or not the City is planning to sell their debt to a third party. Tax lien sale eligible buildings are in greater physical distress than comparable buildings without debt. That outreach funding is crucial, and we saw a real hiccup in the current year. The fiscal year 26 budget did include $2 million for outreach, but the funding was delayed. It was then cut in half when the lien sale was canceled in 2026, and as a result, the coalition members... Can I have a little indulgence? Sure.

So in addition to making sure there is outreach funding in the budget, we also need to make sure that the fiscal year 27 lien sale is not relied on as a revenue generator. It generates a very small amount of revenue in relation to the amount of harm that it causes. I submitted our testimony in writing with a brief from a report we just published two weeks ago that looks at the first and only lien sale that has happened with the reforms that were put in place in 2024. What we learned from on the ground and from the data is that the 2025 lien sale was basically the same in its impact as all of the lien sales that have happened since Rudy Giuliani put that system into place in 1996. We do not want to see the City pass a budget that includes reliance on a lien sale, and we look forward to working with the Council and the administration on a land bank, getting that up and running as quickly as possible so we can have an equitable system in place. Thank you so much.

Magnolia Gardens is an excellent example of what is possible when we invest in keeping families together, providing safe housing where people and their pets can remain together during times of crisis. (06:57:45)

That is an excellent question. So the ASPCA is the contracted provider of affordable spay-neuter through the Animal Population Control Fund. Under this most recent contract, it was assigned fully to us. We provide low-cost or free spay-neuter through that contract to owned pets of low-income individuals, which automatically leaves out the rescue community. We provide services through mobile clinics and three brick-and-mortar clinics. I am going to be very, very honest: in 2025, we provided about 60,000 services and that was nowhere near enough to fill the need. There are many people in the City who are upset that they cannot get an appointment.

(06:58:31)

People joked that it is like trying to get tickets to a Knicks game, where you have to call in at a certain time to make it.

(06:58:35)

So the need far exceeds what we are able to provide. The ASPCA also provides those services free of charge to the rescue community. We also do spay-neuter for the shelter system. Good afternoon, Chair, members of the Finance Committee and Council Finance staff. My name is Sierra Craft and I am the Executive Director of the Eye Care Coalition, a coalition of legal service providers dedicated to ensuring that unaccompanied immigrant children and youth in New York City have access to free legal representation. I wanted to begin by thanking the Council for its leadership and significant investment in immigrant youth through the Unaccompanied Minors and Families Initiative and the SIJS Initiative in Fiscal Year 26. With this funding, seven providers have collectively supported nearly 2,500 unaccompanied children and families in just the first nine months of this fiscal year alone through legal screenings, consultation and full representation. No single organization can meet this need alone, and through your investment we built a coordinated citywide network of 13 providers that connect children to legal services, share resources and expertise, and serve as one of the strongest systems of support for immigrant youth in the country at a time when so many other systems are failing them.

But the reality is that what immigrant youth are facing today is very different than it was just a year ago. Children are being railroaded through an immigration system that is moving faster, becoming more punitive and increasingly forcing — coercing — them to take voluntary departure or receive a removal order, and that is just not really a choice a child should have to make. Additionally, we are seeing judges granting very short continuances of only two weeks rather than the typical four to six months, with no time for youth to obtain counsel or for attorneys to prepare for complex cases. Providers are having to prepare for both the SIJS petition and asylum applications for each youth, so each case is taking much more work and they are having to respond to all these various shifting federal policies. Unaccompanied kids are now detained on an average of six months or more due to increased scrutiny on the sponsors, and providers are working hard to file habeas petitions to challenge prolonged detention.

The work has become more urgent and more complex, and we urge the City Council to fully invest $18.6 million in the Unaccompanied Minors and Families Initiative and fully fund the $2.575 million for the SIJS Initiative. We have built a system that is working and now is not the time to step back from it. Thank you so much for your support.

City Council funding helps us lower or eliminate those barriers for all our immigrant New Yorkers, helping us to assist them with immigration applications, application fees, filing for appeals, filing motions to prevent their removal to a third country and much more. These legal process services go far beyond simply filing one application. Recently, we helped a young person file for SIJS less than a week before his 21st birthday. He had accompanied his younger brother to an appointment and did not realize he was eligible for immigration relief. We helped him file his case in family court on an expedited basis. He received his special findings orders just days before his 21st birthday and we overnighted his application to immigration to arrive before the deadline.

People come to our offices after immigration judges have denied their obligated asylum case. They have not had a full hearing, cannot explain why, or even know the country where they were ordered removed. We help that person figure it out and file an appeal to stand up for the right to have a full and fair hearing. A 2017 Council report found that the cost to maintain adult literacy programs would be $3,700 per student, and yet the most recent literacy contracts had the rate at only $1,300 per student, almost a third of what it would cost to run a program. This undercutting of programs means we are not able to provide adequate staffing for the wrap-around services and counseling that students need outside of their class.
