We believe the agenda should focus both on increasing usage of federally funded food benefits such as SNAP, school breakfast, summer meals and on making groceries more affordable in every neighborhood of the City by using the new municipal grocery stores as home or neighborhood center delivery hubs, testing out mobile markets and instituting a food voucher program that makes a discounted market basket of food available at perhaps thousands of retail locations instead of just five. For this reason we are requesting the following budget items. In order to maximize the federal safety net, we are asking for $5 million for citywide and neighborhood nonprofit organizations to enable their clients to meet the new SNAP able-bodied work requirements. We are asking to fund and baseline, along with many others, the New York City Benefits Program to keep offering targeted benefit enrollment and support. For Hunger Free, we are asking $200,000 for SNAP and emergency food assistance benefits and $75,000 to support increased participation in SNAP. Lastly, we are advocating for $100 million in baseline funding for CFC Food Connection. Thank you.

The Council must allocate sufficient funds to begin implementing the 2023 reform package that would extend the rental assistance program to 50% of the area median income and remove burdensome work requirements. A recent WIN analysis found that if CityFHEPS reforms had been implemented as intended in January 2024, the City could have saved $1 billion in shelter costs alone and cut the family homeless population in half. Two: invest in supportive housing by allocating $78.3 million to renovate a thousand supportive housing units and permanently preserve an additional 325 units. This funding includes a $13 million flexible expense pot to allow repairs to DOHMH units. Three: fund Local Law 35 by investing $40 million to place clinical mental health professionals in every family shelter. This is a critical investment in the long-term health and stability of homeless families. Four: support homeless immigrant families by allocating an additional $85 million for immigration legal services. We must also invest in the SHARE program, which provides a pathway to permanent housing for long-term shelter stayers who are ineligible for other subsidies.

Five: expand workforce development by increasing funding for shelter-based programs like WIN's Income Building Program. Six: uphold the COLA commitment by maintaining the 3% cost of living adjustment for human services workers to ensure that our dedicated workforce can afford to live in the City that they serve. We also urge the Council to pass Int 0450-2026 to advance pay equity among the human services workers in the nonprofit sector. With federal funding uncertain, the City must step up. We urge you to pass a progressive budget that centers the needs of working people and marginalized communities and provides every New Yorker with a fair shot at financial stability and a permanent home. For 30 years we have provided training on social and public benefits and housing programs to social service providers here in the City. We are one of three technical assistance providers in the Benefits initiative, which is designed to expand access to public benefits across the City. In partnership with the other providers, we train and support staff from 35 CBOs to help low-income New Yorkers obtain benefits and achieve greater stability. Since the start of the program in 2023, we have collectively offered 70 trainings. Benefits Plus delivers measurable results, and we believe in this work. SNAP applications submitted with program support are 45% more likely to be approved than those submitted independently. With an annual program cost of Benefits Plus, the program more than pays for itself. In just the first nine months of this fiscal year, Benefits Plus secured over $20 million in annual SNAP benefits for New Yorkers. These benefits support New York families and strengthen our City's economy. The program helps keep vulnerable people connected to benefits and reduces many downstream costs for the City.

While the value of Benefits Plus is crystal clear, it was not included in the executive budget, and I urge you to restore this funding, baseline it and expand it. This work is especially essential in the face of rapidly changing benefits policies. We are currently helping caseworkers respond to SNAP able-bodied work requirements, the ending of the emergency housing vouchers, upcoming Medicaid work rules, elimination of the central plan $200 to $250 and also the approach we take meets the moment. At last week's hearing here, CM Lee, you and CM Hudson spoke with the Commissioner about the need for an all-of-government and all-of-community approach. Benefits Plus is the best way to support New Yorkers. Benefits Plus fosters collaborations between City and nonprofit staff, reaches residents in all 200-plus zip codes in the City and provides compassionate services in 54 languages. Without Benefits Plus, tens of thousands of New Yorkers will lose trusted local support from providers who helped them secure and maintain life-sustaining benefits at a time of major federal changes. The need for this program has never been greater and I urge you to restore and raise Benefits Plus funding in the Fiscal Year 2027 budget, baseline it and invest in its expansion to help more children and families. Alongside over 400 nonprofit staff in this program, I thank you for considering this urgent request.

I remember when police presence became more visible in my school. The environment totally changed. I felt less focused on support and more focused on monitoring and control. For some, school became a place that they wanted to leave as quickly as possible.

One in five New Yorkers is already over the age of 60 and that number is going to rise by 25% by 2040. Yet for decades, the City has underfunded the very programs that help older adults remain in their communities. Without new investment, we risk our centers closing and shrinking services at a time when demand is only increasing. LGBT+ older adults face even greater challenges. Many are isolated, many live alone and are less likely to have family support. In fact, one in four SAGE participants reports that the only person they have to call in an emergency is SAGE. At the same time, they often distrust mainstream systems due to a lifetime of discrimination, including in health care. LGBT+ older adults are twice as likely to report discriminatory treatment. Take Root Justice is the coordinating member of Stabilizing NYC and the coalition seeks an enhancement to $5.3 million to expand the coalition and increase the organizing impact against tenant harassment, displacement and the loss of affordable housing at the hands of predatory equity and speculative investors. We are in a moment of powerful alignment across leadership in City Council and the mayor's office to do portfolio-based tenant organizing, and we need the resources to fuel this work.

Take Root is also a member of the Community Land Trusts Initiative and the consortium seeks an enhancement to $3 million for 23 organizations in Fiscal Year 27. This urgently needed funding will provide essential support for 19 active community land trusts and citywide organizations providing capacity building, training and tailored legal and technical assistance.

We are also members of the Worker Cooperative Business Development Initiative seeking an enhancement. This funding will support the WCBDI coalition's work of starting 30 new small businesses, providing pathways to worker ownership for more than 100 New Yorkers.

In the last few years, the funding has supported our work in representing immigrant workers with claims of sick leave violations, retaliation and wage theft. We requested the Council renew and increase from $2 million in fiscal year 26 to $3 million in fiscal year 27. Low Wage Workers Support funding has remained the same for nearly 10 years and an increase is needed to cover increasing costs and the escalating demand for services. Supporting immigrant workers since fiscal year 25, CILEC have recovered approximately... in unpaid wages and damages. We have advised and represented nearly 1,000 workers and conducted over 50 Know Your Rights trainings. Low Wage Workers Support funding makes this work possible by providing financial recovery, education and empowerment for low wage and immigrant workers.

This crucial funding has lagged behind other priorities, requiring last minute saves from both workers rights advocates and the City Council to renew the funding. We respectfully request the City Council to demonstrate ongoing commitment to the City's low wage immigrant workers by renewing and expanding the Low Wage Workers Support Initiative from $2 million to $3 million. This expansion will stabilize the funding so low wage immigrant workers can continue to receive this essential advocacy through vital civil legal services and community outreach. Failure to renew and expand this funding will have a devastating impact on low wage immigrant workers, leaving them without high quality, culturally sensitive representation in their efforts to obtain economic and worker justice.

Now more than ever, in this dangerous time of attacks on immigrant rights and on our democracy, low wage immigrant workers need champions from advocates like Take Root Justice and our allied CILEC partners. But our ability to provide the crucial administrative infrastructure to continue this life sustaining work requires expanded and sustainable funding. We thank the City Council for its continued support for low wage immigrant workers through this essential funding. Thank you.

I guess CM... I have seen is not here because I was going to thank him personally. Thank you to the Progressive Caucus for your vision and leadership and recognition of the need for preservation.

For decades, New York City has invested hundreds of millions of dollars to create supportive housing for New Yorkers in need of stability. These homes have transformed lives and strengthened families and communities. But like any long term investment, supportive housing requires ongoing care and maintenance to remain a vital part of the City's safety net. Our nonprofits are struggling due to contract rates that have not caught up with rising costs. They lack the capital and offerings support required to make major repairs, improve accessibility for tenants aging in place and retrofit properties so they meet modern energy efficiency standards. Without these new investments, about 20% of existing supportive housing stock is at risk of going offline and staying vacant longer.

At the same time, New York City stands to lose millions in funding in the coming years as the federal government impairs permanent supportive housing through the Continuum of Care program. Providers rely on this previously guaranteed rental assistance to maintain their buildings and units. Without this, 5,000 formerly homeless New Yorkers, including families and survivors, are once again at risk of losing their homes. When the federal government targets evidence based housing solutions and criminalizes our most vulnerable, the City must step up. That is why the Network is urging the Council to invest $78.3 million to preserve 325 supportive housing units and renovate another thousand units this fiscal year. Thank you.

As a former high school student who did not have access to restorative justice services in her school, and who only became deeply ingrained in restorative justice practices because of external programs, I know firsthand how dangerous it is to not have any sort of justice programs in schools.

Last year I testified in front of CM Rita and the Committee on Education about an instance when a student at my high school experienced a mental crisis that involved a knife threat. Instead of seeking a restorative justice or mental health counselor to de-escalate the situation, the administration chose to go on a shutdown and use police force. The presence of police and the overall shutdown scared my classmates, and it especially scared the young person who was going through a crisis, who ended up leaving school. To this day, I believe that if a team of counselors trained in trauma-informed care had approached the student before the police, the student would not have felt the need to flee.

This is not the only instance in public schools where restorative justice or mental health access could have helped young people. This is why it is important to fully fund restorative justice, mental health and immigration services in schools. By freezing the hiring of school police, eliminating vacancies and reinvesting approximately $90 million in savings into restorative justice, mental health supports and immigrant protections, the City will be positively impacting every student and young New Yorker. If the well-being of youth is your goal, then funding restorative justice, the mental health continuum and immigrant protections will be your priority. Thank you.

Good evening. Thank you, Chair and honorable members of the Finance Committee. My name is Theodore, managing attorney for the City Council Program at CUNY Citizenship Now. I am joined by my colleague Alicia Arana, who will speak about our supplemental asylum services program.

Now more than ever, immigrants in the City need our leaders to stand up for their rights. Increased ICE activity, visa bans, prolonged processing times and constant policy changes have created widespread fear and uncertainty. A recent policy memo has significantly restricted access to green cards, while DACA recipients are waiting up to eight months or more for work authorization renewals, causing many to lose their jobs, health insurance and stability through no fault of their own.

CUNY Citizenship Now is a lifeline, providing free, trusted pro se legal immigration services at a time when misinformation and fear are at an all-time high. Founded in 1997 and expanded in 2010 through the City Council program, we serve all 51 City Council districts and have assisted more than 80,000 immigrants through the City Council program alone. This fiscal year we have received 52,000 calls, assisted more than 3,500 and helped 2,225 New Yorkers with their citizenship applications.

We are working closely with Council Members to rebuild trust in communities facing fear and uncertainty in today's immigration landscape. In addition to our one-on-one services that we provide at Council Member offices and designated locations each month, we also hold Saturday citizenship events sponsored by Council Members, along with an annual spring citizenship drive that served nearly 3,000 participants in one day. This year we also introduced monthly Council Member-sponsored virtual consultation days and expanded evening hours to better serve New Yorkers who cannot access services during regular business hours. We have partnered with Council Members on 44 community engagement initiatives, including tabling, presentations and webinars. We have also had Council Members in videos and public information campaigns within our program. The work is powered by a dedicated team of more than 50 attorneys, paralegals and administrators, along with 300 trained volunteers, many of whom have gone through the process themselves and want to give back to the community. Without sustained funding, thousands of immigrants will be left without vital legal support. For fiscal year 2027, we respectfully urge the Council to restore our allocation through the City Council program. Thank you for your leadership and your partnership in supporting immigrant New Yorkers. Thank you.

Across the city, we are increasingly working with individuals and families who are eligible for services but facing many complex barriers to accessing and navigating them in a meaningful way. This is where our model is particularly important — not duplicating existing services but making them work. Case management ensures that individuals and families in refugee and immigrant communities can move through systems efficiently, access what they are already eligible for, find the stability they seek and thrive as part of our city's community.

From our experience responding to displacement in New York and across the country, this kind of early, responsive and strategic support is one of the most effective ways to reduce long-term costs and improve outcomes. When case management is strong, systems function as they are designed to. When it is absent, costs rise across the board and cause avoidable strain on City systems. The $5 million investment proposed is a strategic way to strengthen the City's existing infrastructure so that services already funded translate into real stability and well-being for New Yorkers, and the opportunity to share skills, cultures and dreams that shape our city. New Yorkers deserve a transportation budget that reflects how they actually move through our City and one that meets the urgent safety and accessibility challenges we face today.

The vast majority of New Yorkers walk, bike or take transit, yet our City's investments have not always matched that reality. During the previous administration, the staffing necessary to meet the goals laid out in key City blueprints like the Streets Plan was lacking. New Yorkers were promised safe streets, faster buses and better public spaces, but those promises were often left unmet. We will never know how many New Yorkers were killed or seriously injured because the City did not have the resources it needed. Today is a new era and we are encouraged to see Mayor Mamdani's commitment to invest in safe and world-class streets. We hope that these investments will be sufficient for DOT to meet and exceed the Streets Plan benchmarks, including the unmet targets from the previous administration, to transform our City into one where bus riders are not trapped in traffic, people on bikes have safe and protected rides end to end and pedestrians have more and better sidewalk space.

Among the immediate priorities, two stand out. First, we are calling for $15 million in fiscal year 27 specifically for hardened daylighting, repurposing parking at pedestrian crossings to increase visibility. This could help prevent crashes like those that killed nine-year-old Kamara Hughes and Dolman Adoum. Sadly, just 0.8% of New York City's intersections received hardened daylighting last year. Second, the current $2 million annual allocation for open streets citywide is simply not enough to keep pace with rising costs.

(09:49:36)

And the City must also establish a reliable reimbursement timeline for open streets partners, many of whom are advancing public space improvements with little to no financial support. New Yorkers should not have to personally shoulder the cost of operating public infrastructure. We recognize that this all requires new revenue. Fortunately, we have an opportunity sitting at our doorstep today. Only 2.5% of our parking spaces are metered, far below peer cities. Demand-based metered parking on just 25% of eligible commercial corridors would better manage our curb space while generating as much as $4 billion annually for neighborhood improvements. The greatest city in the world deserves the greatest streets in the world, and that requires sustained, ambitious investment. By increasing dedicated funding to DOT and pairing it with smart revenue solutions, New York City can build a transportation system for the future, not one catching up to the past. Thank you.

Good evening, City Council. Thank you for your time.

It will fund community-based organizations already serving LGBTQ+ immigrants, expand access to safe housing, health care and services, and ensure that immigrant legal services are culturally competent, language accessible and explicitly affirming of LGBT immigrants. The proposal demands $7.5 million to create a new Speaker-allocated fund for City discretionary funding and $7.5 million to expand the Mayor's Office of Immigrant Affairs' existing services by adding additional LGBTQ-specialized attorneys and social services.

LGBT immigrants live at the intersections of invisibility and terror. We have fled places where our existence is criminalized. Hello. Good morning — oh, actually I guess good evening. My name is Amanda Jules. I am a parent of a sixth grader at the School of the Future Manhattan. Thank you for listening.

Today I want to discuss the City's commitment to our children's future. I am aware the Council cannot reverse a procurement award and this testimony does not ask it to, but I do have three requests. At the end of the COMPASS commitment came with a clear rationale. At the May 28th budget hearing, the Commissioner stated that increased investment represents stability for the sector by supporting stronger staffing structures and better wages. The numbers tell a different story. Manhattan Youth Recreation, who lost their COMPASS contract at 12 schools, currently pays activity specialists $44 an hour to start versus $21 to $24 an hour at New York Junior Tennis League, who obtained those schools. They pay program directors hovering around $85,000 annually versus $65,000 to $75,000 annually at Image and Foundation, who obtained four of their schools. They pay group leaders $22 to $25 an hour versus $17 at the YMCA, who obtained two of their schools and is one of the largest nonprofits in the state. Apologies, I did not turn my paper.

If the aforementioned were central objectives, why are providers not being held to advance these goals? The research itself cites the importance of relationships and lasting connections with caring adults as foundational. Middle school is exactly when that consistency matters most. When the provider changes, that continuity is lost. There is also a structural equity gap. High school team sports are funded by the City. Middle school sports are funded entirely by CBOs. Manhattan Youth is a funding partner. A 2022 research study published in PLOS ONE from a national study of more than 11,000 youth found that participation in team sports is directly associated with fewer mental health difficulties.

Families selecting a middle school in this City are not choosing a building — they are choosing a culture, and the after-school program is part of that. After middle school selection deadlines left families without advertised programs. The procurement process gave sole discretion under Attachment 2 to bypass the highest scoring proposal, limited awards by geography, set school preferences as advisory and determined how many schools any provider received based on undefined programmatic distribution, with no standards governing how that discretion was applied. This led to middle schools receiving their lowest preference provider, some schools receiving a provider they have been trying to get rid of, and other schools not receiving a provider who mutually wanted to return. While discretion may be legally permissible and appropriate in other procurement contexts, after-school programming is not a conventional market. The stakes are children's stability and well-being, not vendor competition. This method requires a higher standard of transparency and child-centered justification.

As a step towards transparency, evaluator identities should be public and future audits conducted against awarded connections to check for any underlying bias. The New York City Council's own December 2025 report card gave a D in service delivery to New Yorkers as well as in relationships and collaboration. This procurement ran inside that agency under that framework. This is not a Manhattan Youth-specific problem. It is a system problem visible in the data across one set of contracts. The Council should ask whether it is present across other higher-value procurement.

My first ask: Saturday Night Lights, which the Council baselined in the fiscal year 2026 budget for 136 locations — is the precedent of a City baseline for that program the same kind of action correcting the same kind of grievous inequity that should apply to the level of after-school programming where it is currently missing? Two: attach terms and conditions to the fiscal year 2027 after-school appropriation requiring an independent audit of the award process, with contract extensions for existing providers at transitional schools during the audit duration. And lastly, require that future assignments be published preceding middle school selection deadlines, as many families consider after-school programming in school choice.

The money has already been committed. I am asking the Council to help ensure that what was promised is actually delivered. Thank you. Good evening.

(11:11:53)

Good evening, committee members, and thank you for the opportunity to testify. My name is Holden Alan Berg and I am a high school student assisting a PhD candidate with research in urban economics. I also have legislative experience with CM Brewer and Assembly Member Linda Rosenthal. Over the past few months I have been helping develop a memo examining the cost of shifting New York City's waste system towards a more sustainable composting model. When I began this work I expected to learn where New York City's waste goes. Instead I found myself asking a different question: why are we spending so much money throwing value away?

According to the Independent Budget Office, organic material makes up more than one third of New York City's residential waste stream, yet only about 2.4% of residential waste collected in 2025 was captured through organics collection programs. At the same time, New York City businesses generate roughly 4 million tons of waste annually and an estimated significant portion of food waste is still sent to landfills each year. When discussions focus only on the price of expanding composting infrastructure, it is easy to overlook the ongoing costs of transporting and disposing of millions of tons of potentially valuable material year after year.

For many New Yorkers the cost of waste management is largely invisible. It appears somewhere in taxes, service fees and municipal budgets, but those costs are real. Every dollar spent transporting organic material to a landfill is a dollar that cannot be invested elsewhere. At a time when families are already struggling with the cost of groceries, rent and everyday necessities, New Yorkers deserve a system that makes better use of the resources they are already paying for. According to City budget analysis, it costs over a billion dollars a year to ship waste out of the City. According to a Brooklyn Borough President report on expanding compost, the City spends over $195 million per year moving food scraps alone on their way to a landfill. The question is whether we will keep paying to discard that material or begin investing in a system that recovers value from it. Delay is not neutral — it is a continuation of that cost compounded over time.

I am here because we will all inherit both the environmental and economic consequences of today's budget decisions. I urge the Council to continue investing in organics collection, composting infrastructure, public education and the integration of organics into the City's broader waste management reforms. Thank you for your time and consideration.

I am a New York native, a professional rail infrastructure project manager with over five years of experience and an incoming graduate student in transport and city planning at University College London. I am here to testify regarding the short-sighted allocation of $43 million for the Queensway Metropolitan Hub within the capital budget.

As a rail engineer I must state clearly: this design represents an act of permanent option value destruction for outer borough infrastructure. Just so we are clear, the Mayor has stated that the administration is moving forward with Queensway in a way that does not preclude future right-of-way activation. As an infrastructure project manager I must challenge the internal logic of that statement. By placing permanent park structures directly over the existing track bed of the Rockaway Beach Branch, the City is at the very least spending many millions to structurally block a vital north-south transit corridor.

I urge the community to consider three critical financial and spatial realities. First, the choice between parks and transit is a false one. The Mayor acts as if we must simply wait and do nothing while seeking state-level interest, but the City does not have to wait. The Queens Link proposal offers a collocated rails and trails design. By simply shifting the pedestrian trail to the side of the embankment, the City can deliver a continuous greenway today while explicitly protecting the rail bed for future transit use.

Secondly, Queensway has zero provisions to accommodate rail, yet is being built in an area already surrounded by existing park space and greenway. Conversely, South Queens, a severe transit desert, has a park-only budget that completely ignores working-class families who endure grueling multi-seat commutes just to travel a few miles within their own borough.

Third, we must reject the inflated cost padding. The administration has argued that the cost of a park is minimal compared to route expansion, but $43 million. Time expired. It is not a minor amount. It is a significant capital allocation that denies preservation of the right of way. While the park's soft costs are rejected, a $6 billion price tag from independent engineering audits has confirmed the actual civil construction costs at $1.8 billion. It is financially irresponsible to fast-track a multibillion-

(11:59:35)

dollar project while utilizing park numbers that shortchange the other boroughs. The budget should reflect our true values. A park is a localized luxury. A transit line is an economic lifeline. I am calling on this community to pause on the Metropolitan Hub funding and mandate a joint-use design that preserves the rail corridor. Let us focus on a budget that protects rails and includes parks.

So, dear Chair Personally and members of the Finance Committee, my name is Alexi Sanoff and I am a longtime transit advocate from New York City and an urban planning student at the University of Michigan. I am speaking in strong support of the Queens Link project, which would bring both new subway service and new parkland to Queens by reactivating the former Rockaway Beach Branch, and against the current $43 million allocated in the budget for phase one of the Queens Way, also referred to as the Met Hub project.

Two competing concepts exist for the deactivated Rockaway Beach Branch right of way: the park-only Queens Way and the dual-purpose Queens Link, which combines subway and park uses. Renderings produced by the Queens Way show that their proposed park would be built directly on the former rail bed, imposing a massive obstacle to future rail reactivation. There are virtually no examples of rail lines with parks being reconverted back into rail lines. Famously, Atlanta's Belt Rail and Trails proposal built the trail first and never got around to building the rail. The rail must be designed for and built in from the start.

Full construction and integration of Queens Link would create the first north-south subway line in Queens, extending the M service south from Rego Park to Ozone Park and then over the A train...

(12:26:14)

tracks to the Rockaways. It could ultimately increase through service to the Rockaways by up to 65%. This in turn would open terminal capacity in Forest Hills for the G train to operate along the Queens Boulevard local tracks, increasing local service by around 50% and connecting central Queens to northern and western Brooklyn.

A study by the Steer Group found that the Queens Link proposal would replace millions of vehicle trips every year, making streets safer and lowering harmful emissions. This makes it, ironically, the greener option. It does this while adding up to 33 acres of new parks and trails integrated with the subway design. Over 20 years, the study concluded the project would more than pay for itself by creating jobs, saving New Yorkers time and reducing health care costs tied to air pollution and traffic crashes.
